Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… thanks. I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to actually make the call—through the employee assistance program—was my sleep. It’s been really messed up.

I’ll get into bed and I’m exhausted, like I should just be able to pass out, but then my brain kind of turns on. I’m lying there replaying stuff from work—conversations, things I should’ve handled differently, numbers, meetings—and then it shifts to arguments at home or things I said, things I shouldn’t have said. It’s like I can’t get any distance from it once the lights are off.

And even when I do fall asleep, I keep waking up early, usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. Then I just lie there, knowing I have to be up soon, which makes it worse. By the time the day starts, I feel like I technically slept, but not in a way that actually counts. Like I never really rested. That’s what made me think, okay, maybe I need to talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.

I’m not totally sure how to separate the two, honestly. During the day I’m definitely tense sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d call it excessive worry in the same way it happens at night.

Like, at work I’m busy enough that I can usually just keep moving from one thing to the next, and I don’t always notice what I’m thinking. It’s more when things get quiet that I realize how wound up I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day panicking or constantly worrying about everything, I don’t think so. Not exactly. It feels more like I’m carrying stress around, and then it catches up with me later.

Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.

A lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.

And then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.

I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as traumatic, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant or stressful?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I avoid something, it’s usually more because I don’t have the energy for it or I don’t want another difficult conversation, not because it’s tied to some particular memory. I haven’t noticed any pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I mean, I have normal little routines, like checking my calendar a few times or rereading an email before I send it, especially for work, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck repeating things over and over where I can’t stop.\n\nWith thoughts, I guess I can get caught on something if it’s bothering me, but it doesn’t feel like a specific repeated thought I have to do or some ritual or anything. It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.

Like I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”

And I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.

And it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.

I don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.

Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.

I guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.

And honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.

I’m lying there replaying stuff from work—conversations, things I should’ve handled differently, numbers, meetings—and then it shifts to arguments at home or things I said, things I shouldn’t have said. It’s like I can’t get any distance from it once the lights are off.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I keep waking up early, usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. Then I just lie there, knowing I have to be up soon, which makes it worse. By the time the day starts, I feel like I technically slept, but not in a way that actually counts. Like I never really rested. That’s what made me think, okay, maybe I need to talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive worry or anxiety during the day, when you're not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate the two, honestly. During the day I’m definitely tense sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d call it excessive worry in the same way it happens at night.\n\nLike, at work I’m busy enough that I can usually just keep moving from one thing to the next, and I don’t always notice what I’m thinking. It’s more when things get quiet that I realize how wound up I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day panicking or constantly worrying about everything, I don’t think so. Not exactly. It feels more like I’m carrying stress around, and then it catches up with me later.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or distressing events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean when they ask that. Like, I haven’t been through something where I’d say, “That was a major trauma,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful periods, obviously—work stuff, family arguments, just life getting heavy sometimes—but nothing that stands out as, you know, a single terrible event or something I keep reliving. At least not that I can point to. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as traumatic, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant or stressful?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I avoid something, it’s usually more because I don’t have the energy for it or I don’t want another difficult conversation, not because it’s tied to some particular memory. I haven’t noticed any pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I mean, I have normal little routines, like checking my calendar a few times or rereading an email before I send it, especially for work, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck repeating things over and over where I can’t stop.\n\nWith thoughts, I guess I can get caught on something if it’s bothering me, but it doesn’t feel like a specific repeated thought I have to do or some ritual or anything. It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really… I don’t think so. When you say “specific behavior,” I’m thinking like I suddenly have to check something, or repeat something, or do some exact thing to make the feeling go away, and I don’t think that’s what happens.

It’s more like I get really uncomfortable and I want out of the thought. So I might try to distract myself, or I’ll kind of argue with it in my head, or I’ll look for something else to focus on. I mean, the distress can feel pretty strong, and I do want relief from it. But I haven’t noticed some overwhelming urge to perform a particular action. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.

I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. When you say “specific behavior,” I’m thinking like I suddenly have to check something, or repeat something, or do some exact thing to make the feeling go away, and I don’t think that’s what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I get really uncomfortable and I want out of the thought. So I might try to distract myself, or I’ll kind of argue with it in my head, or I’ll look for something else to focus on. But it doesn’t feel like there’s one behavior I’m driven to do, like, “If I don’t do this, I can’t calm down.”\n\nI mean, the distress can feel pretty strong, and I do want relief from it. But I haven’t noticed some overwhelming urge to perform a particular action. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They feel unwelcome, yeah. I mean, I’m not sitting down thinking, “Let me thoughtfully examine whether I’ve been a good husband or father.” It doesn’t feel that calm or intentional.

It’s more like something catches in my head and then it just starts running. I’ll remember one late night at work, and then it’s like my brain starts pulling up every other example it can find — all the nights I stayed late, the tailgates, the dumb purchases, little decisions that maybe I didn’t think were a big deal at the time. And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.

I don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.
=====================================

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks. I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to actually make the call—through the employee assistance program—was my sleep. I’m lying there replaying stuff from work—conversations, things I should’ve handled differently, numbers, meetings—and then it shifts to arguments at home or things I said, things I shouldn’t have said. It’s like I can’t get any distance from it once the lights are off.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I keep waking up early, usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. Then I just lie there, knowing I have to be up soon, which makes it worse. By the time the day starts, I feel like I technically slept, but not in a way that actually counts. Like I never really rested. That’s what made me think, okay, maybe I need to talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive worry or anxiety during the day, when you're not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate the two, honestly. During the day I’m definitely tense sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d call it excessive worry in the same way it happens at night.\n\nLike, at work I’m busy enough that I can usually just keep moving from one thing to the next, and I don’t always notice what I’m thinking. It’s more when things get quiet that I realize how wound up I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day panicking or constantly worrying about everything, I don’t think so. Not exactly. It feels more like I’m carrying stress around, and then it catches up with me later.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or distressing events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean when they ask that. Like, I haven’t been through something where I’d say, “That was a major trauma,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful periods, obviously—work stuff, family arguments, just life getting heavy sometimes—but nothing that stands out as, you know, a single terrible event or something I keep reliving. At least not that I can point to. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as traumatic, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant or stressful?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I avoid something, it’s usually more because I don’t have the energy for it or I don’t want another difficult conversation, not because it’s tied to some particular memory. I haven’t noticed any pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I mean, I have normal little routines, like checking my calendar a few times or rereading an email before I send it, especially for work, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck repeating things over and over where I can’t stop.\n\nWith thoughts, I guess I can get caught on something if it’s bothering me, but it doesn’t feel like a specific repeated thought I have to do or some ritual or anything. It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or do certain mental acts to try to balance out or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is asking about compulsive mental acts or neutralizing thoughts used to balance out distressing thoughts, which is more directly related to OCD-type compulsions and is not covered by the provided Major Depressive Disorder symptom profile items."}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. When you say “specific behavior,” I’m thinking like I suddenly have to check something, or repeat something, or do some exact thing to make the feeling go away, and I don’t think that’s what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I get really uncomfortable and I want out of the thought. So I might try to distract myself, or I’ll kind of argue with it in my head, or I’ll look for something else to focus on. But it doesn’t feel like there’s one behavior I’m driven to do, like, “If I don’t do this, I can’t calm down.”\n\nI mean, the distress can feel pretty strong, and I do want relief from it. But I haven’t noticed some overwhelming urge to perform a particular action. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. I mean, I’m not sitting down thinking, “Let me thoughtfully examine whether I’ve been a good husband or father.” It doesn’t feel that calm or intentional.\n\nIt’s more like something catches in my head and then it just starts running. I’ll remember one late night at work, and then it’s like my brain starts pulling up every other example it can find — all the nights I stayed late, the tailgates, the dumb purchases, little decisions that maybe I didn’t think were a big deal at the time. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of having to think a particular thought to cancel it out or balance it. I don’t have something like, “If I think I let them down, then I have to think this other exact thing three times,” or anything like that.

What I do is more messy than that. I’ll try to talk myself down, like, “You’ve done good things too,” or “You’re making this bigger than it is,” but it doesn’t feel like some mental act I’m required to do to make it okay. It’s more like I’m trying to survive the feeling in the moment, if that makes sense.

Sometimes I’ll go over examples in my head, almost like I’m building a case for or against myself, but even that doesn’t really feel like I’m trying to neutralize the thought. It feels more like I’m stuck arguing with myself and hoping I’ll land somewhere less awful. But no, I haven’t noticed a specific thought or mental routine I feel forced to do to make up for it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks. I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to actually make the call—through the employee assistance program—was my sleep. It’s been really messed up.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’m exhausted, like I should just be able to pass out, but then my brain kind of turns on. I’m lying there replaying stuff from work—conversations, things I should’ve handled differently, numbers, meetings—and then it shifts to arguments at home or things I said, things I shouldn’t have said. It’s like I can’t get any distance from it once the lights are off.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I keep waking up early, usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. Then I just lie there, knowing I have to be up soon, which makes it worse. By the time the day starts, I feel like I technically slept, but not in a way that actually counts. Like I never really rested. That’s what made me think, okay, maybe I need to talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive worry or anxiety during the day, when you're not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate the two, honestly. During the day I’m definitely tense sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d call it excessive worry in the same way it happens at night.\n\nLike, at work I’m busy enough that I can usually just keep moving from one thing to the next, and I don’t always notice what I’m thinking. It’s more when things get quiet that I realize how wound up I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day panicking or constantly worrying about everything, I don’t think so. Not exactly. It feels more like I’m carrying stress around, and then it catches up with me later.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or distressing events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean when they ask that. Like, I haven’t been through something where I’d say, “That was a major trauma,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful periods, obviously—work stuff, family arguments, just life getting heavy sometimes—but nothing that stands out as, you know, a single terrible event or something I keep reliving. At least not that I can point to. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as traumatic, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant or stressful?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I avoid something, it’s usually more because I don’t have the energy for it or I don’t want another difficult conversation, not because it’s tied to some particular memory. I haven’t noticed any pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I mean, I have normal little routines, like checking my calendar a few times or rereading an email before I send it, especially for work, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck repeating things over and over where I can’t stop.\n\nWith thoughts, I guess I can get caught on something if it’s bothering me, but it doesn’t feel like a specific repeated thought I have to do or some ritual or anything. It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I must, no. I mean, I know I sort of already touched on this, but the word “must” feels stronger than what I experience.

When those thoughts come up, I do sometimes try to think my way out of them, like reminding myself of times I was there for my family, or telling myself I’m being too harsh. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a specific thought I have to think to undo it, like some exact phrase or mental step that makes it right again.

It’s more like I’m uncomfortable and I’m searching around in my own head for something that will make it hurt less. Sometimes I’ll go back and forth for a while, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that I’m not as bad as the thought says I am. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.

So… no, not really. I don’t have a set thought or mental thing I feel forced to do to make up for it. It’s more just me getting stuck in it and trying to talk myself down.

It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. I’ll remember one late night at work, and then it’s like my brain starts pulling up every other example it can find — all the nights I stayed late, the tailgates, the dumb purchases, little decisions that maybe I didn’t think were a big deal at the time. And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or do certain mental acts to try to balance out or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of having to think a particular thought to cancel it out or balance it. I don’t have something like, “If I think I let them down, then I have to think this other exact thing three times,” or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do is more messy than that. I’ll try to talk myself down, like, “You’ve done good things too,” or “You’re making this bigger than it is,” but it doesn’t feel like some mental act I’m required to do to make it okay. It’s more like I’m trying to survive the feeling in the moment, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll go over examples in my head, almost like I’m building a case for or against myself, but even that doesn’t really feel like I’m trying to neutralize the thought. It feels more like I’m stuck arguing with myself and hoping I’ll land somewhere less awful. But no, I haven’t noticed a specific thought or mental routine I feel forced to do to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to try to undo or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I must, no. I mean, I know I sort of already touched on this, but the word “must” feels stronger than what I experience.\n\nWhen those thoughts come up, I do sometimes try to think my way out of them, like reminding myself of times I was there for my family, or telling myself I’m being too harsh. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.\n\nSo… no, not really. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.\n\nSo… no, not really. I don’t have a set thought or mental thing I feel forced to do to make up for it. It’s more just me getting stuck in it and trying to talk myself down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are affecting your ability to work or interact with others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / sales_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up, but my team has had to cover gaps when my forecasts and client prep slip, and at home I’m less present with my wife and kids than I used to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think they are, although I keep wanting to say “not completely,” because I’m still functioning on paper. I’m still showing up to work, I’m still in meetings, I’m still answering emails and doing the job enough that it’s not like I’ve disappeared.

But it’s definitely affecting things. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my work depends on staying ahead of stuff — forecasts, client prep, making sure my team has what they need before a call or a deadline. And lately those are the things that slip. I’ll be sitting there trying to prepare for a client meeting and my head will drift back into this whole thing about how I’m failing at home, or I’ll start second-guessing myself and lose time. Then suddenly I’m scrambling, or I haven’t given the team a clean forecast, or someone else has to jump in and cover a gap I should’ve handled.

It’s embarrassing, because I don’t think of myself as someone who drops the ball like that. My team has been good about it, but I can tell they’ve had to pick up the slack here and there, and that just feeds the whole loop, honestly.

And at home… I’m there, but I’m not always really there. My wife or the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I only half-heard them because I’m stuck in my own head. I still do the basic things, but I’m less present with them than I used to be. That’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because the thoughts are about letting them down, and then being distracted by the thoughts makes me feel like I’m doing exactly that.

---------- turn 14 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks. I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to actually make the call—through the employee assistance program—was my sleep. It’s been really messed up.\n\nI’ll get into bed and I’m exhausted, like I should just be able to pass out, but then my brain kind of turns on. I’m lying there replaying stuff from work—conversations, things I should’ve handled differently, numbers, meetings—and then it shifts to arguments at home or things I said, things I shouldn’t have said. It’s like I can’t get any distance from it once the lights are off.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I keep waking up early, usually around 3 or 4 in the morning. Then I just lie there, knowing I have to be up soon, which makes it worse. By the time the day starts, I feel like I technically slept, but not in a way that actually counts. Like I never really rested. That’s what made me think, okay, maybe I need to talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience excessive worry or anxiety during the day, when you're not trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate the two, honestly. During the day I’m definitely tense sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know that I’d call it excessive worry in the same way it happens at night.\n\nLike, at work I’m busy enough that I can usually just keep moving from one thing to the next, and I don’t always notice what I’m thinking. It’s more when things get quiet that I realize how wound up I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day panicking or constantly worrying about everything, I don’t think so. Not exactly. It feels more like I’m carrying stress around, and then it catches up with me later.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly traumatic or distressing events in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people usually mean when they ask that. Like, I haven’t been through something where I’d say, “That was a major trauma,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, there have been stressful periods, obviously—work stuff, family arguments, just life getting heavy sometimes—but nothing that stands out as, you know, a single terrible event or something I keep reliving. At least not that I can point to. I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as traumatic, but nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of something unpleasant or stressful?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I don’t think I’m avoiding places or people because they remind me of something specific. There are definitely things I don’t feel like doing lately, or people I might put off dealing with if I know it’s going to be stressful, but it doesn’t feel like, “I can’t go there because it brings something back,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike if I avoid something, it’s usually more because I don’t have the energy for it or I don’t want another difficult conversation, not because it’s tied to some particular memory. I haven’t noticed any pattern like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I imagine you mean. I mean, I have normal little routines, like checking my calendar a few times or rereading an email before I send it, especially for work, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck repeating things over and over where I can’t stop.\n\nWith thoughts, I guess I can get caught on something if it’s bothering me, but it doesn’t feel like a specific repeated thought I have to do or some ritual or anything. It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. When you say “specific behavior,” I’m thinking like I suddenly have to check something, or repeat something, or do some exact thing to make the feeling go away, and I don’t think that’s what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I get really uncomfortable and I want out of the thought. So I might try to distract myself, or I’ll kind of argue with it in my head, or I’ll look for something else to focus on. But it doesn’t feel like there’s one behavior I’m driven to do, like, “If I don’t do this, I can’t calm down.”\n\nI mean, the distress can feel pretty strong, and I do want relief from it. But I haven’t noticed some overwhelming urge to perform a particular action. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. I mean, I’m not sitting down thinking, “Let me thoughtfully examine whether I’ve been a good husband or father.” It doesn’t feel that calm or intentional.\n\nIt’s more like something catches in my head and then it just starts running. I’ll remember one late night at work, and then it’s like my brain starts pulling up every other example it can find — all the nights I stayed late, the tailgates, the dumb purchases, little decisions that maybe I didn’t think were a big deal at the time. And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or do certain mental acts to try to balance out or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of having to think a particular thought to cancel it out or balance it. I don’t have something like, “If I think I let them down, then I have to think this other exact thing three times,” or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do is more messy than that. I’ll try to talk myself down, like, “You’ve done good things too,” or “You’re making this bigger than it is,” but it doesn’t feel like some mental act I’m required to do to make it okay. It’s more like I’m trying to survive the feeling in the moment, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll go over examples in my head, almost like I’m building a case for or against myself, but even that doesn’t really feel like I’m trying to neutralize the thought. It feels more like I’m stuck arguing with myself and hoping I’ll land somewhere less awful. But no, I haven’t noticed a specific thought or mental routine I feel forced to do to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to try to undo or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I must, no. I mean, I know I sort of already touched on this, but the word “must” feels stronger than what I experience.\n\nWhen those thoughts come up, I do sometimes try to think my way out of them, like reminding myself of times I was there for my family, or telling myself I’m being too harsh. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a specific thought I have to think to undo it, like some exact phrase or mental step that makes it right again.\n\nIt’s more like I’m uncomfortable and I’m searching around in my own head for something that will make it hurt less. Sometimes I’ll go back and forth for a while, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that I’m not as bad as the thought says I am. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.\n\nSo… no, not really. I don’t have a set thought or mental thing I feel forced to do to make up for it. It’s more just me getting stuck in it and trying to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are affecting your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are, although I keep wanting to say “not completely,” because I’m still functioning on paper. I’m still showing up to work, I’m still in meetings, I’m still answering emails and doing the job enough that it’s not like I’ve disappeared.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my work depends on staying ahead of stuff — forecasts, client prep, making sure my team has what they need before a call or a deadline. And lately those are the things that slip. I’ll be sitting there trying to prepare for a client meeting and my head will drift back into this whole thing about how I’m failing at home, or I’ll start second-guessing myself and lose time. Then suddenly I’m scrambling, or I haven’t given the team a clean forecast, or someone else has to jump in and cover a gap I should’ve handled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I don’t think of myself as someone who drops the ball like that. My team has been good about it, but I can tell they’ve had to pick up the slack here and there, and that just feeds the whole loop, honestly.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but I’m not always really there. My wife or the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I only half-heard them because I’m stuck in my own head. I still do the basic things, but I’m less present with them than I used to be. That’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because the thoughts are about letting them down, and then being distracted by the thoughts makes me feel like I’m doing exactly that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or do certain mental acts to try to balance out or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of having to think a particular thought to cancel it out or balance it. I don’t have something like, “If I think I let them down, then I have to think this other exact thing three times,” or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do is more messy than that. I’ll try to talk myself down, like, “You’ve done good things too,” or “You’re making this bigger than it is,” but it doesn’t feel like some mental act I’m required to do to make it okay. It’s more like I’m trying to survive the feeling in the moment, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll go over examples in my head, almost like I’m building a case for or against myself, but even that doesn’t really feel like I’m trying to neutralize the thought. It feels more like I’m stuck arguing with myself and hoping I’ll land somewhere less awful. But no, I haven’t noticed a specific thought or mental routine I feel forced to do to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to try to undo or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I must, no. I mean, I know I sort of already touched on this, but the word “must” feels stronger than what I experience.\n\nWhen those thoughts come up, I do sometimes try to think my way out of them, like reminding myself of times I was there for my family, or telling myself I’m being too harsh. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a specific thought I have to think to undo it, like some exact phrase or mental step that makes it right again.\n\nIt’s more like I’m uncomfortable and I’m searching around in my own head for something that will make it hurt less. Sometimes I’ll go back and forth for a while, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that I’m not as bad as the thought says I am. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.\n\nSo… no, not really. I don’t have a set thought or mental thing I feel forced to do to make up for it. It’s more just me getting stuck in it and trying to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are affecting your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are, although I keep wanting to say “not completely,” because I’m still functioning on paper. I’m still showing up to work, I’m still in meetings, I’m still answering emails and doing the job enough that it’s not like I’ve disappeared.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my work depends on staying ahead of stuff — forecasts, client prep, making sure my team has what they need before a call or a deadline. And lately those are the things that slip. I’ll be sitting there trying to prepare for a client meeting and my head will drift back into this whole thing about how I’m failing at home, or I’ll start second-guessing myself and lose time. Then suddenly I’m scrambling, or I haven’t given the team a clean forecast, or someone else has to jump in and cover a gap I should’ve handled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I don’t think of myself as someone who drops the ball like that. My team has been good about it, but I can tell they’ve had to pick up the slack here and there, and that just feeds the whole loop, honestly.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but I’m not always really there. My wife or the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I only half-heard them because I’m stuck in my own head. I still do the basic things, but I’m less present with them than I used to be. That’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because the thoughts are about letting them down, and then being distracted by the thoughts makes me feel like I’m doing exactly that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think they are. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because I am still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m doing the meetings, I’m answering people. From the outside it probably looks like I’m mostly handling it.

There have been moments where someone else has had to step in or cover a gap because I wasn’t as prepared as I should’ve been, or I didn’t get something lined up clearly enough ahead of time. And that really bothers me, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping those things moving, not creating extra work for other people.

And at home, it’s similar in a different way. I’m physically there with my wife and kids, but I’m less present than I used to be. Like, we’ll be sitting together or they’ll be talking to me, and part of me is somewhere else in my head. I still care, obviously, and I want to enjoy that time, but it’s harder to just relax into it. Then I feel bad because I’m missing moments I should be there for.

So… yeah. I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely making it harder to enjoy my family and do my job the way I know I can.
