It’s more just… I’ll think about the same problem because it’s unresolved. But no, I haven’t really noticed repetitive actions or thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you throughout the day, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that does happen. Not like a random phrase or anything, but it’s more like this loop of… I’ll be trying to work, or driving, or even just sitting at home, and my mind starts going back over all the ways I feel like I’ve messed things up.\n\nA lot of it is about my family. I’ll replay every late night I stayed at work, like I chose that over being home, even if at the time I told myself it was necessary. Or I’ll think about stupid things I spent money on—dumb purchases that seemed harmless then—and it turns into this whole thing in my head where I’m like, “Why did I do that? Why am I like this?” Even little stuff, like tailgating or staying out longer than I should have, it starts feeling like evidence that I’m selfish.\n\nAnd then the thought that keeps coming back is basically that my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down. I know that probably sounds dramatic, and I can sometimes tell myself it’s not that simple, but when it comes up, it feels pretty convincing. It’ll just keep circling back, even when I’m trying to focus on something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are popping into your head without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, let me think about how I’ve failed my family for a while.” It just sort of shows up.\n\nLike I’ll be doing something totally normal, and suddenly I’m replaying another late night at work, thinking about how many times I chose to stay there instead of being home. Or I’ll remember some tailgate or some stupid thing I spent money on, and it starts as one memory but then it kind of snowballs. Before I know it, I’m thinking, “Of course, that’s what I do. I put myself first. My wife and kids are stuck with this selfish guy who keeps letting them down.”\n\nAnd I don’t really want to be thinking that. I mean, part of me feels like I deserve to think about it, if that makes sense, but it’s not intentional. It just pops in and then I have a hard time getting away from it once it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts away when they come up, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do try to push them away, or at least I try to argue with them or distract myself. Like I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop, this isn’t helpful,” or I’ll try to focus on work or turn on the radio or look at something else, but it’s like they keep finding their way back in.\n\nAnd it’s usually the same kind of chain. I’ll think about one late night at work, and then suddenly I’m replaying every time I stayed late instead of being home. Then it turns into tailgates, dumb purchases, all these choices that maybe weren’t huge on their own, but in my head they add up into this picture of me being selfish. Like my wife and kids got stuck with someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to sit there thinking that. I really don’t. But when I try to shove it away, it’ll quiet down for a little bit and then come back from another angle, like, “No, but remember this too?” So yeah, I guess I am trying to push it away, but it doesn’t really stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something or think something specific to make these thoughts about letting your family down go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I have to do something specific, exactly. It’s not like there’s a ritual or a certain phrase I repeat and then it goes away.\n\nI guess what I do is more like… I try to reason with myself, or I try to distract myself. I’ll tell myself, “You’re not helping anything by going over this again,” or I’ll try to focus harder on whatever I’m supposed to be doing. Sometimes I’ll kind of mentally review the situation, like, “Okay, was that actually as bad as I’m making it?” But that doesn’t really feel like a specific thing I have to do. It’s more just me trying to get some distance from it.\n\nAnd honestly, a lot of the time I’m not even sure I’m doing it in a structured way. It’s just uncomfortable, so I want it to stop, and my mind starts grabbing at anything that might make it quiet down. But no, I don’t think there’s one particular action or thought that I feel forced to do to make it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an overwhelming urge to perform a specific behavior to reduce your distress when these thoughts about letting your family down come up?\n\nPatient: Not really… I don’t think so. When you say “specific behavior,” I’m thinking like I suddenly have to check something, or repeat something, or do some exact thing to make the feeling go away, and I don’t think that’s what happens.\n\nIt’s more like I get really uncomfortable and I want out of the thought. So I might try to distract myself, or I’ll kind of argue with it in my head, or I’ll look for something else to focus on. But it doesn’t feel like there’s one behavior I’m driven to do, like, “If I don’t do this, I can’t calm down.”\n\nI mean, the distress can feel pretty strong, and I do want relief from it. But I haven’t noticed some overwhelming urge to perform a particular action. It’s more just… I want the thoughts to stop, and I’m kind of fumbling around trying to get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are unwelcome and intrusive, or are they something you're intentionally reflecting on?\n\nPatient: They feel unwelcome, yeah. I mean, I’m not sitting down thinking, “Let me thoughtfully examine whether I’ve been a good husband or father.” It doesn’t feel that calm or intentional.\n\nIt’s more like something catches in my head and then it just starts running. I’ll remember one late night at work, and then it’s like my brain starts pulling up every other example it can find — all the nights I stayed late, the tailgates, the dumb purchases, little decisions that maybe I didn’t think were a big deal at the time. And then it all stacks up into this ugly conclusion that my wife and kids got stuck with someone selfish, someone who keeps letting them down.\n\nI don’t want to be thinking that. I’m not choosing it in the way I’d choose to reflect on something useful. It just kind of barges in, and then once it’s there, it’s hard not to follow it. Sometimes I tell myself, “Okay, maybe you should think about this and learn from it,” but honestly it doesn’t feel productive. It feels more like I’m punishing myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think certain thoughts or do certain mental acts to try to balance out or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the sense of having to think a particular thought to cancel it out or balance it. I don’t have something like, “If I think I let them down, then I have to think this other exact thing three times,” or anything like that.\n\nWhat I do is more messy than that. I’ll try to talk myself down, like, “You’ve done good things too,” or “You’re making this bigger than it is,” but it doesn’t feel like some mental act I’m required to do to make it okay. It’s more like I’m trying to survive the feeling in the moment, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll go over examples in my head, almost like I’m building a case for or against myself, but even that doesn’t really feel like I’m trying to neutralize the thought. It feels more like I’m stuck arguing with myself and hoping I’ll land somewhere less awful. But no, I haven’t noticed a specific thought or mental routine I feel forced to do to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to try to undo or make up for the thoughts about letting your family down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I must, no. I mean, I know I sort of already touched on this, but the word “must” feels stronger than what I experience.\n\nWhen those thoughts come up, I do sometimes try to think my way out of them, like reminding myself of times I was there for my family, or telling myself I’m being too harsh. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a specific thought I have to think to undo it, like some exact phrase or mental step that makes it right again.\n\nIt’s more like I’m uncomfortable and I’m searching around in my own head for something that will make it hurt less. Sometimes I’ll go back and forth for a while, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that I’m not as bad as the thought says I am. But I don’t feel like if I don’t think the “right” thing, something terrible happens or the thought somehow isn’t undone.\n\nSo… no, not really. I don’t have a set thought or mental thing I feel forced to do to make up for it. It’s more just me getting stuck in it and trying to talk myself down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are affecting your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are, although I keep wanting to say “not completely,” because I’m still functioning on paper. I’m still showing up to work, I’m still in meetings, I’m still answering emails and doing the job enough that it’s not like I’ve disappeared.\n\nBut it’s definitely affecting things. I’m a sales manager, so a lot of my work depends on staying ahead of stuff — forecasts, client prep, making sure my team has what they need before a call or a deadline. And lately those are the things that slip. I’ll be sitting there trying to prepare for a client meeting and my head will drift back into this whole thing about how I’m failing at home, or I’ll start second-guessing myself and lose time. Then suddenly I’m scrambling, or I haven’t given the team a clean forecast, or someone else has to jump in and cover a gap I should’ve handled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I don’t think of myself as someone who drops the ball like that. My team has been good about it, but I can tell they’ve had to pick up the slack here and there, and that just feeds the whole loop, honestly.\n\nAnd at home… I’m there, but I’m not always really there. My wife or the kids will be talking to me and I’ll realize I only half-heard them because I’m stuck in my own head. I still do the basic things, but I’m less present with them than I used to be. That’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because the thoughts are about letting them down, and then being distracted by the thoughts makes me feel like I’m doing exactly that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are making it harder for you to enjoy time with your loved ones or perform your job to the best of your ability?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think they are. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because I am still showing up. I’m going to work, I’m doing the meetings, I’m answering people. From the outside it probably looks like I’m mostly handling it.\n\nBut I’m not doing it as well as I normally would. The big thing at work is that my forecasts and client prep have been slipping. Not every time, but enough that I notice it, and my team notices it. There have been moments where someone else has had to step in or cover a gap because I wasn’t as prepared as I should’ve been, or I didn’t get something lined up clearly enough ahead of time. And that really bothers me, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping those things moving, not creating extra work for other people.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar in a different way. I’m physically there with my wife and kids, but I’m less present than I used to be. Like, we’ll be sitting together or they’ll be talking to me, and part of me is somewhere else in my head. I still care, obviously, and I want to enjoy that time, but it’s harder to just relax into it. Then I feel bad because I’m missing moments I should be there for.\n\nSo… yeah. I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely making it harder to enjoy my family and do my job the way I know I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are in line with how you see yourself as a person?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s kind of the part that messes with me, honestly. On one hand, I don’t want to think of myself as a selfish person. If you asked me on a better day, I’d probably say I’m someone who tries hard, who cares about his family, who wants to provide and be there.\n\nBut when these thoughts get going, it starts to feel like maybe that’s just the version of myself I want to believe. I’ll replay things over and over — every late night I stayed at work, every tailgate or thing I chose for myself, every dumb purchase that in hindsight feels irresponsible — and I start putting it all together like evidence. Like, “See? This is who you are. Your wife and kids got stuck with a guy who keeps choosing himself, or keeps dropping the ball, and they’re paying for it.”\n\nAnd I know that sounds harsh. Part of me can step back and say, okay, people work late, people spend money poorly sometimes, people aren’t perfect. But emotionally it doesn’t land that way. It lands more like… I’m this selfish guy who keeps letting them down, even if I didn’t mean to.\n\nSo I guess the answer is complicated. It’s not in line with how I want to see myself. But lately, when I’m stuck in it, it starts to feel like it might be the truth about me, and that’s really hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are significantly interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “significantly” is the word I keep getting stuck on, because I’m not at the point where I’ve stopped going to work or I’m not talking to my family. I’m still doing the day-to-day stuff. I’m still showing up, still getting through meetings, still answering emails, still getting dinner with my wife and kids and being in the house with them.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, yes, it is interfering. At work, I can tell I’m not as sharp or as ahead of things as I usually am. My forecasts have slipped, and my client prep isn’t always as solid as it should be. And because I’m in a manager role, when I miss something, it doesn’t just affect me — it means my team has to cover gaps or scramble a little, and that makes me feel even worse. I hate that, because part of my job is supposed to be making their lives easier, not adding to the load.\n\nAnd with my family, it’s not that I’m absent exactly, but I’m less present. My wife or the kids will be talking, and I’ll realize I’m only half there because I’m stuck thinking about how I’m failing them. So I’m technically in the room, but not really connected the way I want to be. That has been happening more than I’d like to admit.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s interfering. I’m still functioning, but it’s definitely getting in the way of work and how I am with my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are causing you a lot of emotional pain or distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It hurts a lot, honestly.\n\nIt’s not just like a passing thought where I go, “Oh, I could’ve handled that better,” and then move on. It turns into this whole loop in my head. I’ll start replaying things — staying late at work when I probably should’ve been home, going to a tailgate or doing something for myself, buying something stupid that now feels irresponsible — and I kind of stack them all up like proof against myself.\n\nAnd then the thought becomes, “This is what your wife and kids got stuck with.” Like they got stuck with a selfish guy who keeps letting them down, who keeps choosing the wrong thing, even if at the time I didn’t mean it that way. And I know that probably sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nThat’s the painful part. It’s not just guilt over one thing. It’s like I start questioning what kind of husband and father I am. And once I’m in that place, it’s hard to shake. I can be sitting there with them and still feel this heavy feeling, like I don’t deserve the life I have with them because I’m not doing right by them.\n\nSo, yeah… it causes a lot of distress. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about letting your family down are something you want to think about, or are they unwelcome and intrusive?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely unwelcome. I don’t sit down and think, “Let me spend the next hour beating myself up about my family.” It’s not like that at all. I don’t want to think about it.\n\nIt just kind of barges in, usually from something small. Like I’ll remember a night I stayed late at work, or I’ll think about going to a tailgate instead of being home, or I’ll see some purchase and think, “Why did I spend money on that?” And then suddenly it’s not just that one thing anymore. It turns into this whole list in my head, like I’m building a case against myself.\n\nAnd before I know it, I’m back at the same place: that my wife and kids got stuck with a selfish guy who keeps letting them down. I hate that thought. I don’t want it. But once it starts, it feels hard to push away, because part of me starts treating it like it’s true, even though another part of me knows it’s not helpful to keep going over it.\n\nSo, yeah… intrusive is a good word. It feels like it comes in without me inviting it, and then it just keeps digging.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… I don’t think they’re in line with what I actually believe, at least not when I’m thinking clearly.

I mean, I do value being a good husband and father. That’s probably why it hits me so hard. I care a lot about providing for them and being there and not being selfish.
